3
Preliminary tools

This section gathers preliminary results used to build the minimax GAN estimators of the following sections. These tools are both of statistical nature (Section
3.1
), and of analytical nature (Section
3.2
).

3.1
Bias-variance trade-off for GANs

In this Section, we suppose that we are in the setting of Model
1
(which includes the setting of Model
3
). We exhibit a general bound on the objective and give the required values of the approximation errors and covering numbers of the classes
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{D}
, so that this bound provides minimax rates.

3.1.1
A general bound

Consider two closed sets of functions: a set of generators
\mathcal{G}\subset\mathcal{H}^{\beta+1}_{K}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{p})
and a set of discriminators
\mathcal{D}\subset\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}_{1}(B^{p}(0,K),\mathbb{R})
. The approximation error of the class
\mathcal{G}
is defined as

\Delta_{\mathcal{G}}:=\inf\limits_{g\in\mathcal{G}}d_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\gamma}}(g_{\#U},g^{\star}_{\#U}).

(3.1)

The error
\Delta_{\mathcal{G}}
corresponds to how well the class
\mathcal{G}
approximates
g^{\star}_{\#U}
for the cost
d_{\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}_{1}}
. It is crucial for this error to be small since

\Delta_{\mathcal{G}}\leq\mathbb{E}_{X_{i}\sim g^{\star}_{\#U}}\big[d_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\gamma}}(\hat{g}_{\#U},g^{\star}_{\#U})\Big].

On the other hand, the approximation error of the class
\mathcal{D}
is defined as

\Delta_{\mathcal{D}}:=\sup\limits_{g\in\mathcal{G}}\bigl\{d_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\gamma}}(g_{\#U},g^{\star}_{\#U})-d_{\mathcal{D}}(g_{\#U},g^{\star}_{\#U})\bigl\}.

(3.2)

It corresponds to how well the class
\mathcal{D}
approximates the discrimination made by
\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}_{1}
between the measures
g^{\star}_{\#U}
and
g_{\#U}
, for all
g\in\mathcal{G}
. As opposed to
\Delta_{\mathcal{G}}
, it is not crucial that this error be small. Indeed the role of the class
\mathcal{D}
is only to associate a score
d_{\mathcal{D}}(g_{\#U},g^{\star}_{\#U})
to each function
g\in\mathcal{G}
, in order to find a good potential minimizer
\hat{g}_{\#U}
. However, this score does not have to be at the same scale as
d_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\gamma}}(g_{\#U},g^{\star}_{\#U}).

For example, we show in Section
5
that under suitable assumptions on
g,g^{\star}
we have

d_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\gamma}}(g_{\#U},g^{\star}_{\#U})\leq d_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\beta+1}}(g_{\#U},g^{\star}_{\#U})^{\frac{\beta+\gamma}{2\beta+1}}

up to logarithmic factors. Therefore, having a class
\mathcal{D}
that well approximates
\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\beta+1}
allows to obtain a minimax estimator although
\Delta_{\mathcal{D}}
is big in this case. Nevertheless, if
\Delta_{\mathcal{D}}
is small, it ensures that the discrimination done by the class
\mathcal{D}
is relevant and therefore with high probability,
\hat{g}
is a good candidate among the class
\mathcal{G}
.

Let us now define the notion of covering number which is the other feature of
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{D}
that intervenes in the bound on the objective. For
\epsilon>0
, a minimal
\epsilon
-covering
\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}
of a class of functions
\mathcal{F}
is defined as:

\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}:=\argmin\{|A|\ |\ \forall f\in\mathcal{F},\exists f_{\epsilon}\in A,\|f-f_{\epsilon}\|_{\infty}\leq\epsilon\}.

(3.3)

The covering numbers
|\mathcal{G}_{\epsilon}|
and

|\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}|
characterise the sizes of the classes of functions. The larger the classes are, the most likely the estimators
\hat{g}
and
\hat{D}_{\hat{g}}
(see Definition
B.5
) will overfit the data
(X_{i})_{i}
. The following result exhibits a classical bias/variance trade off between the approximation errors and covering numbers.

Theorem 3.1
.

If
g^{\star}\in\mathcal{H}^{\beta+1}_{K}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{p})
and
\mathcal{G}\subset\mathcal{H}^{\beta+1}_{K}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{p})
,
\mathcal{D}\subset\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}_{1}(B^{p}(0,K),\mathbb{R})
, then the GAN estimator (
1.3
) verifies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}

\displaystyle\Big[d_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\gamma}}(\hat{g}_{\#U},g^{\star}_{\#U})\Big]-\Delta_{\mathcal{G}}-\Delta_{\mathcal{D}}

\displaystyle\leq C\min\limits_{\delta\in[0,1]}\Biggl\{\sqrt{\frac{(\delta+1/n)^{2}\log(n|\mathcal{G}_{1/n}||\mathcal{D}_{1/n}|)}{n}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}(1+\delta^{(1-\frac{d}{2\gamma})}+\log(\delta^{-1})\mathds{1}_{\{2\gamma=d\}})\Biggl\}.

The proof of Theorem
3.1
can be found in Section
B.2.1
.
It gives a bound on the error of the estimator in terms of covering number and approximation errors of the classes
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{D}
.
It is a WGAN version of Theorem 1 in
Puchkin et al. 2024
, designed for Jensen-Shannon GANs.

3.1.2
Expected growth of optimal generators and discriminators classes

Let us discuss the values of the approximation errors and covering numbers that are required by Theorem
3.1
to achieve minimax optimality. From
Tang and Yang 2023
, we know that the minimax convergence rate is
O(n^{-\frac{\beta+\gamma}{2\beta+d}}\vee n^{-\frac{1}{2}})
. Therefore, if
\frac{\beta+\gamma}{2\beta+d}<1/2
, in light of Theorem
3.1
we need to take
\delta\geq Cn^{-\frac{\gamma}{2\beta+d}}
so that
\frac{\delta^{1-\frac{d}{2\gamma}}}{\sqrt{n}}\leq Cn^{-\frac{\beta+\gamma}{2\beta+d}}
. Then, we also need

\log(|\mathcal{G}_{1/n}|)\vee\log(|\mathcal{D}_{1/n}|)\leq Cn^{\frac{d}{2\beta+d}}

(3.4)

so that
\delta\sqrt{\frac{\log(|\mathcal{G}_{1/n}||\mathcal{D}_{1/n}|)}{n}}\leq Cn^{-\frac{\beta+\gamma}{2\beta+d}}\vee n^{-\frac{1}{2}}
. Lastly, we need to take
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{D}
such that

\Delta_{\mathcal{G}}\vee\Delta_{\mathcal{D}}\leq C\left(n^{-\frac{\beta+\gamma}{2\beta+d}}\vee n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right).

(3.5)

On the other hand, we can prove that the covering number of a class of functions is lower bounded by its precision of approximation of the Hölder classes.

Proposition 3.2
.

Let be
\eta,\epsilon>0
and
\mathcal{F}\subset L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathbb{R})
a class of functions such that

\sup\limits_{f\in\mathcal{H}^{\eta}_{1}}\ \inf\limits_{g\in\mathcal{F}}\|f-g\|_{\infty}\leq\epsilon
,
then for all
\theta\in(0,\epsilon/3)
we have

\log(|\mathcal{F}_{\theta}|)\geq C\epsilon^{-\frac{d}{\eta}}.

The proof of Proposition
3.2
can be found in Section
B.2.2
. From the previous proposition we see that although we need the approximation errors of the classes
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{D}
to verify
\Delta_{\mathcal{G}}\vee\Delta_{\mathcal{D}}\leq Cn^{-\frac{\beta+\gamma}{2\beta+d}}\vee n^{-\frac{1}{2}}
in order to be minimax, we also need

\sup\limits_{g^{\star}\in\mathcal{H}^{\beta+1}_{K}}\ \inf\limits_{g\in\mathcal{G}}\|g^{\star}-g\|_{\infty}\geq Cn^{-\frac{\beta+1}{2\beta+d}}\quad\text{ and }\quad\sup\limits_{D^{\star}\in\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}_{1}}\ \inf\limits_{D\in\mathcal{D}}\|D^{\star}-D\|_{\infty}\geq Cn^{-\frac{\gamma}{2\beta+d}\frac{d}{p}}

(3.6)

so that
\log(|\mathcal{G}_{1/n}|)\vee\log(|\mathcal{D}_{1/n}|)\leq Cn^{\frac{d}{2\beta+d}}
as desired. We are going to build classes of functions verifying these properties using a wavelet approximation produced by neural networks.

3.2
Wavelets: a key tool to describe regularity

In this section, we introduce the wavelet framework
(
Giné and Nickl 2015
)
and use it to build the classes
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{D}
of our GAN estimator in the setting of Model
1
.

3.2.1
Wavelets, Besov spaces and regularity trade-off

Let
\psi,\phi\in\mathcal{H}^{\lfloor\beta\rfloor+3}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R})
be a compactly supported
scaling
and
wavelet
function respectively (see
(
Daubechies 1988
)
on Daubechies wavelets). For ease of notation, the functions
\psi,\phi
will be written
\psi_{0},\psi_{1}
respectively. Then for
j\in\mathbb{N}_{0},l\in\{1,...,2^{p}-1\},w\in\mathbb{Z}^{p}
, the family of functions

\psi_{0w}(x)=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{p}\psi_{0}(x_{i}-w_{i})\ \text{ , }\ \psi_{jlw}(x)=2^{jp/2}\prod\limits_{i=1}^{p}\psi_{l_{i}}(2^{j}x_{i}-w_{i})

form an orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{p},\mathbb{R})
(with
l_{i}
the
i
th digit of the base-2-decomposition of
l
).
Let
q_{1},q_{2}\geq 1,s>0
and
b\geq 0
such that
\lfloor\beta\rfloor+3>s
. The Besov space
\mathcal{B}^{s,b}_{q_{1},q_{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{p},\mathbb{R})
consists of functions
f:\mathbb{R}^{p}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
that admit a wavelet expansion in
L^{2}
:

f(x)=\sum\limits_{w\in\mathbb{Z}^{p}}\alpha_{f}(w)\psi_{0w}(x)+\sum\limits_{j=0}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{l=1}^{2^{p}-1}\sum\limits_{w\in\mathbb{Z}^{p}}\alpha_{f}(j,l,w)\psi_{jlw}(x),

equipped with the norm

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}^{s,b}_{q_{1},q_{2}}}=

\displaystyle\Biggl(\left(\sum\limits_{w\in\mathbb{Z}^{p}}|\alpha_{f}(w)|^{q_{1}}\right)^{q_{2}/q_{1}}

\displaystyle+\sum\limits_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{jq_{2}(s+p/2-p/q_{1})}(1+j)^{bq_{2}}\sum\limits_{l=1}^{2^{p}-1}\Big(\sum\limits_{w\in\mathbb{Z}^{p}}|\alpha_{f}(j,l,w)|^{q_{1}}\Big)^{q_{2}/q_{1}}\Biggl)^{1/q_{2}}.

Note that for
b=0
,
\mathcal{B}^{s,0}_{q_{1},q_{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{p},\mathbb{R})
coincides with the classical Besov space
\mathcal{B}^{s}_{q_{1},q_{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{p},\mathbb{R})

(
Giné and Nickl 2015
)
.
The Besov spaces can be generalized for any
s\in\mathbb{R}
as a subspace of the space of tempered distribution
\mathcal{S}^{{}^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{p})
. Indeed for
f\in\mathcal{S}^{{}^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{p})
and
C>0
, writing
\alpha_{f}(j,l,w)=\langle f,\psi_{jlw}\rangle
, the Besov space for
s\in\mathbb{R}
is defined as

\mathcal{B}^{s,b}_{q_{1},q_{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{p},\mathbb{R},C)=\{f\in\mathcal{S}^{{}^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{p})|\ \|f\|_{\mathcal{B}^{s,b}_{q_{1},q_{2}}}\leq C\}.

In the following we will use intensively the connection between Hölder and Besov spaces.

Lemma 3.3
.

(Proposition 4.3.23
(
Giné and Nickl 2015
)
, (4.63)
(
Haroske 2006
)
) If
\alpha>0
is a non integer, then

\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{p},\mathbb{R})=\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}_{\infty,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{p},\mathbb{R})

with equivalent norms. If
\alpha\geq 0
is an integer, then
\forall\epsilon>0

\mathcal{B}^{\alpha,1+\epsilon}_{\infty,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{p},\mathbb{R})\xhookrightarrow{}\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{p},\mathbb{R})\xhookrightarrow{}\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}_{\infty,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{p},\mathbb{R}),

where we write
A\xhookrightarrow{}B
if the function space
A
compactly injects in the function space
B
.

The
p
-dimensional wavelet basis
(\psi_{jlw})_{jlw}
will allow us to describe functions in
\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{p},\mathbb{R})
that appear in the
d_{\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}_{1}}
IPM. On the other hand, to describe our generators
g\in\mathcal{G}\subset\mathcal{H}^{\beta+1}_{K}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{p})
, we will use the periodised wavelet basis (see
Giné and Nickl 2015
). One can construct a wavelet basis on
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
by using the periodised scaling and wavelet functions on
[0,1]/\mathbb{Z}
:

\phi^{per}_{j}(s)=2^{j/2}\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\phi(2^{j}(s-k))\text{ and }\psi^{per}_{j}(s)=2^{j/2}\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\psi(2^{j}(s-k)).

(3.7)

Note that it is a well known fact that
\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\phi(s-k)=1
(Section 4.3.4 in
Giné and Nickl 2015
). For ease of notation,
\phi^{per}_{j}
and
\psi_{j}^{per}
will be written
\psi^{per}_{j0},\psi^{per}_{j1}
. Then

\displaystyle\Big(1,\big\{\psi^{per}_{jlz}=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{d}\psi^{per}_{jl_{i}}(\cdot-2^{-j}z_{i})\ |\ j\in\mathbb{N}_{0},l\in\{1,...,2^{d}\},z\in\{0,...,2^{j}-1\}^{d}\big\}\Big),

is a wavelet basis of
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
. We will use this basis to describe functions in
\mathcal{H}^{\beta+1}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{p})
as Lemma
3.3
still holds for the periodised Besov spaces

\mathcal{B}^{\beta+1}_{\infty,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{p})
(Section 4.3.4 in
Giné and Nickl 2015
). Let us define an operator that allows to change the regularity of a function by modifying its wavelet coefficients.

Definition 3.4
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be either
\mathbb{R}^{\eta}
or the torus
[0,1]^{\eta}/\mathbb{Z}^{\eta}
, and
s,b,\gamma,c\in\mathbb{R}
. For
f\in\mathcal{B}^{s,b}_{\infty,\infty}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R})
a tempered distribution, define the tempered distribution
\Gamma^{\gamma,c}(f)\in\mathcal{B}^{s+\gamma,b+c}_{\infty,\infty}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R})
by its wavelets coefficients

\langle\Gamma^{\gamma,c}(f),\psi_{jlz}\rangle:=2^{j\gamma}(1+j)^{c}\langle f,\psi_{jlz}\rangle.

The tempered distribution
\Gamma^{\gamma,c}(f)
is a "regularization" of
f
if
\gamma<0
or
\gamma=0
and
c<0
. For ease of notation, we will write
\Gamma^{\gamma}
instead of
\Gamma^{\gamma,0}
. A first use of this operator is in the following lemma.

Lemma 3.5
.

For all
\eta\geq 1
,
g\in\mathcal{H}^{\eta}_{K}
,
\gamma\in[1,\eta+1]
and
\epsilon\in(0,1)
we have

d_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\gamma}}(g_{\#U},g^{\star}_{\#U})\leq C\left(\log(\epsilon^{-1})^{1/2}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{p}\|\Gamma^{-(\gamma-1)}(g_{i})-\Gamma^{-(\gamma-1)}(g^{\star}_{i})\|_{L^{2}([0,1]^{d},\mathbb{R})}+\epsilon\right).

The proof of Lemma
3.5
can be found in Section
B.3.1
. This lemma asserts that the distance between
g_{\#U}
and
g^{\star}_{\#U}
for the norm
d_{\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}_{1}}
is controlled by the
L^{2}
distance between the
\gamma-1
regularization of
g
and
g^{\star}
. Therefore, to have a small generator error
\Delta_{\mathcal{G}}
, we do not have to control
\|g-g^{\star}\|_{\infty}
, but only the quantity
\|\Gamma^{-(\gamma-1),-2}(g)-\Gamma^{-(\gamma-1),-2}(g^{\star})\|_{L^{2}}
. This is a key result that will help us obtain the right bound on
\Delta_{\mathcal{G}}
. Indeed, from (
3.5
) we know that we need
\Delta_{\mathcal{G}}\leq Cn^{-\frac{\beta+\gamma}{2\beta+d}}\vee n^{-\frac{1}{2}}
, but from (
3.6
) we know that we will have at best
\inf\limits_{g\in\mathcal{G}}\|g^{\star}-g\|_{\infty}\geq Cn^{-\frac{\beta+1}{2\beta+d}}
. Therefore, we need to take advantage of the
\gamma
-smoothness of the discriminator in order to obtain

\Delta_{\mathcal{G}}=\inf\limits_{g\in\mathcal{G}}d_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\gamma}}(g_{\#U},g^{\star}_{\#U})\leq Cn^{-\frac{\beta+\gamma}{2\beta+d}}\vee n^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

Let us note that in
Tang and Yang 2023
it is supposed that the target measure
\mu
belongs to the class
\mathcal{S}
used in the adversarial training (see Section
2.3
), which allows them to get around this problem as they have
\Delta_{\mathcal{S}}=\inf_{\nu\in\mathcal{S}}d_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\gamma}}(\nu,\mu)=d_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\gamma}}(\mu,\mu)=0
.
Furthermore, to utilize the
\gamma
-smoothness of the discriminator, they compute a high order estimator of the type

\mathcal{J}(D)=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\sum\limits_{1\leq|j|\leq\lfloor\gamma\rfloor}\frac{1}{j!}\nabla^{j}D(X_{i})\big(\hat{g}\circ\hat{q}(X_{i})-X_{i}\big)^{j}

which makes them having to compute all the derivatives up to the order
\gamma
of every
D\in\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{p},\mathbb{R})
. Lemma
3.5
gives a simple way to utilize the
\gamma
-smoothness of the discriminator using wavelet theory and that allows us not to have to compute high order estimators.

To obtain Lemma
3.5
, the main idea is to write

\mathbb{E}\Big[D(g(U))-D(g^{\star}(U))\Big]=\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{1}\Big\langle\nabla D\big(g^{\star}(U)+t(g(U)-g^{\star}(U))\big),\ g(U)-g^{\star}(U)\Big\rangle dt\Big],

and to transfer the regularity of the discriminator
\nabla D\in\mathcal{H}^{\gamma-1}_{1}
to the generators
g,g^{\star}
by modifying their wavelets coefficients. This result is a particular case of Proposition
B.1
that shows that in a general setting, we can trade the regularity of the distributions in the
L^{2}
scalar product. This result will be used multiple times along the paper.

3.2.2
From GANs to wavelets and back

Let us now discuss how we are going to use wavelet theory to construct our classes of generators and discriminators. For
\eta\in(0,\beta+1),\delta\in(0,1)
, and
C_{\eta}>0
such that
\mathcal{H}^{\eta}_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{p})\subset\mathcal{B}^{\eta}_{\infty,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{p},C_{\eta})
, define

\mathcal{F}^{\eta,\delta}_{per}:=\{f\in\mathcal{B}^{\eta}_{\infty,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{p},C_{\eta}K)|\ \langle f_{i},\psi_{jlz}^{per}\rangle_{L^{2}}=0,\forall j\geq\log_{2}(\delta^{-1})\}

(3.8)

the set of functions in
\mathcal{B}^{\eta}_{\infty,\infty}
that do not have frequencies higher than
\log_{2}(\delta^{-1})
.
We will later build our class of generators
\mathcal{G}
as an approximation by neural network of the class
\mathcal{F}^{\eta,\delta}_{per}
.
From Lemma
3.5
, approximation error and covering numbers of the class
\mathcal{F}^{\eta,\delta}_{per}
can be derived.

Proposition 3.6
.

We have

\Delta_{\mathcal{F}^{\eta,\delta}_{per}}:=\sup\limits_{f^{\star}\in\mathcal{H}^{\eta}_{K}}\inf\limits_{f\in\mathcal{F}^{\eta,\delta}_{per}}d_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\gamma}}(f_{\#U},f^{\star}_{\#U})\leq C\log(\delta^{-1})^{C_{2}}\delta^{\eta-1+\gamma},

and
\forall\epsilon>0
,

\log(|\mathcal{F}^{\eta,\delta}_{per}|_{\epsilon})\leq C\delta^{-d}\log(\delta^{-1})\log(\epsilon^{-1}).

The proof of Proposition
3.6
can be found in Section
B.3.2
. Choosing
\delta=\delta_{n}:=n^{-\frac{1}{2\beta+d}}
and
\eta=\beta+1
in Proposition
3.6
, we obtain

\Delta_{\mathcal{F}^{\beta+1,\delta_{n}}_{per}}\leq C\log(n)^{C_{2}}n^{-\frac{\beta+\gamma}{2\beta+d}}\quad\text{ and }\quad\log(|\mathcal{F}^{\beta+1,\delta_{n}}_{per}|_{1/n})\leq Cn^{\frac{d}{2\beta+d}}\log(n)^{2}.

(3.9)

From (
3.4
) and (
3.5
), we see that these properties make
\mathcal{F}^{\beta+1,\delta_{n}}_{per}
ideal to be our class of generators
\mathcal{G}
.

To make the method tractable,
we do not use the class
\mathcal{F}^{\beta+1,\delta_{n}}_{per}
in our estimator but rather a neural networks class that allows to approximate it. These neural networks can be either with tanh
(
De Ryck et al. 2021
)
or ReQU activation functions
(
Belomestny et al. 2023
)
. Fix
L>0
and let be
\hat{\phi}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that

\|\phi-\hat{\phi}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{\lfloor\beta\rfloor+2}}\leq CL^{-1},

(3.10)

for
\phi
the Daubechies scaling function. The existence of a neural network (either with tanh or ReQU activation)
\hat{\phi}
satisfying (
3.10
) is guaranteed by Lemmas
A.1
and
A.2
in Section
A.2.1
. We also detail in this section how to compute
\hat{\psi}^{per}_{jlz}
(
A.10
), the approximation by neural networks of the periodised Daubechies wavelet
\psi^{per}_{jlz}
using
\hat{\phi}
. Define the class of function
\hat{\mathcal{F}}^{\eta,\delta}_{per}
by

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{F}}^{\eta,\delta}_{per}=\Big\{f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{p})|

\displaystyle\ f_{i}=\sum\limits_{j=0}^{\log_{2}(\delta^{-1})}\sum\limits_{l=1}^{2^{d}}\sum\limits_{z\in\{0,...,2^{j}-1\}^{d}}\hat{\alpha}(j,l,w)_{i}\hat{\psi}^{per}_{jlz}

\displaystyle\text{ with }|\hat{\alpha}(j,l,z)_{i}|\leq C_{\eta}K2^{-j(\eta+d/2)},\forall i\in\{1,...,p\}\Big\},

(3.11)

for
C_{\eta}
the constant such that
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\eta}_{\infty,\infty}}\leq C_{\eta}\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{H}^{\eta}}
. The class
\hat{\mathcal{F}}^{\eta,\delta}_{per}
is an approximation of the class
\mathcal{F}^{\eta,\delta}_{per}
defined in (
3.8
) using the tanh (or ReQU) neural network approximation
\hat{\psi}^{per}_{jlz}
(
A.10
) of the periodised wavelet (
3.7
). Using propositions
3.6
we obtain in the next proposition that
\hat{\mathcal{F}}^{\eta,\delta}_{per}
inherits the same properties as
\mathcal{F}^{\eta,\delta}_{per}
in terms of approximation error and covering numbers.

Proposition 3.7
.

If
\hat{\phi}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is such that (
3.10
) holds with
L\geq\delta^{-(\lfloor\beta\rfloor+1+\gamma)}
, then

\Delta_{\hat{\mathcal{F}}^{\eta,\delta}_{per}}:=\sup\limits_{f^{\star}\in\mathcal{H}^{\eta}_{K}}\inf\limits_{f\in\hat{\mathcal{F}}^{\eta,\delta}_{per}}d_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\gamma}}(f_{\#U},f^{\star}_{\#U})\leq C\log(\delta^{-1})^{C_{2}}\delta^{\eta+\gamma-1}

and
\forall\epsilon\in(\delta^{\eta-1}L^{-1},1)
,

\log(|\hat{\mathcal{F}}^{\eta,\delta}_{per}|_{\epsilon})\leq C\delta^{-d}\log(\lfloor\delta^{-1}\rfloor)\log(\epsilon^{-1}).

The proof of Proposition
3.7
can be found in Section
B.3.3
. Using this result, we have from (
3.9
) that the class
\hat{\mathcal{F}}^{\beta+1,n^{-\frac{1}{2\beta+d}}}_{per}
is perfectly suited to be our class of generators.

The choice of the discriminator class is however more delicate as we do not have a result such as Lemma
3.5
enabling to exploit the
(\beta+1)
-smoothness of the generators to bound
\Delta_{\mathcal{D}}
(
3.2
).
Indeed, we used Lemma
3.5
to show that we can transfer the regularity of the discriminators to the generators in the IPM
d_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\gamma}}(g_{\#U},g^{\star}_{\#U})
, and therefore bound
\Delta_{\mathcal{G}}
by the approximation error of the
\beta+\gamma
-smooth map
\Gamma^{-(\gamma-1)}(g^{\star})
, for the
L^{2}
distance. We will see in the following sections that it is not straightforward that we can do the same thing for
\Delta_{\mathcal{D}}
(meaning giving the regularity of generators to discriminators) as the measures
g_{\#U}
and
g^{\star}_{\#U}
do not have any regularity from the point of view of the
p
-dimensional Lebesgue measure.

In the next section, we study two different settings to get insights on how to solve this problem. We show that we can either bypass it by taking a non tractable class
\mathcal{D}
, or add more assumptions on
g^{\star}
(Model
3
) so that the measure
g^{\star}_{\#U}
has some regularity from the point of view of the
p
-dimensional Lebesgue measure.

4
Model generality vs. tractability: two sides models

In this section we treat two different settings to get some insights on how to build a minimax and tractable GAN estimator. We first treat the general case of Model
1
and discuss the limits of the assumptions. Then, we treat the case of Model
2
to understand in the easier setting of full dimension, how additional assumptions can help us obtain a tractable estimator.

4.1
Model
1
: A tractable generator class in the general low dimensional case

In this section, we fix a
\gamma\in[1,\beta+1]
and define two classes of function
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{D}
such that the associated GAN estimator is minimax for
d_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\gamma}}
in the setting of Model
1
.

Fix the approximation of the scaling function in (
3.10
) to be at precision
L^{-1}=n^{-1}
and take as the generator class

\mathcal{G}:=\hat{\mathcal{F}}^{\beta+1,n^{-\frac{1}{2\beta+d}}}_{per}.

(4.1)

It is a neural network architecture (with either tanh or ReQU activation) composed of a core block
\hat{\psi}^{per}
(trained to approximate the periodised wavelet
\psi^{per}
) and a final layer that outputs a linear combination of the
O(n^{d}\log(n))
blocks
\hat{\psi}^{per}(2^{j}\cdot-w_{i})
.
The core block
\hat{\psi}^{per}
has width
O(n)
, depth
O(1)
, and the linear layer is of size
O(n^{d}\log(n))
.
In particular, (
4.1
) is a subclass of the classical fully-connected architecture with width
O(n^{d+1}\log(n))
and depth
O(1)
.
We detail in Section
A.2.2
how to implement the class (
4.1
) in practice.

Let us take as the discriminator class

\mathcal{D}:=\{D_{g,g^{{}^{\prime}}}^{\star}|\ g,g^{{}^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{G}_{1/n}\},

(4.2)

with
D^{\star}_{g,g^{{}^{\prime}}}\in\argmax\limits_{D\in\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}_{1}}\mathbb{E}[D(g(U))-D(g^{{}^{\prime}}(u))]
. The class of discriminators is constructed specifically to well approximate the IPMs between the measures
g_{\#U}
with
g\in\mathcal{G}
, without being too massive. This class
\mathcal{D}
is not really computable in practice, but we use it here to illustrate that we can bypass the problems risen by the lack of regularity of the target measure
g^{\star}_{\#U}
, at the cost of the tractability of the method. Having defined the classes
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{D}
, we can state the final bound for the GAN estimator (
1.3
) in the setting of Model
1
.

Theorem 4.1
.

For all
g^{\star}\in\mathcal{H}^{\beta+1}_{K}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{p})
and
\gamma\in[1,\beta+1]
, the GAN estimator
\hat{g}
of (
1.3
) with
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{D}
defined in (
4.1
) and (
4.2
), satisfies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[d_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\gamma}}(\hat{g}_{\#U},g^{\star}_{\#U})\Big]\leq C\log(n)^{C_{2}}\left(n^{-\frac{\beta+\gamma}{2\beta+d}}\vee n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right).

The proof of Theorem
4.1
can be found in Section
C.1.1
. As usual in nonparametric estimation, an extra logarithmic term in Theorem
4.1
is to be a attributed to a concentration bound (Theorem
3.1
). More interestingly, another one arises from functional inequalities when
\beta
is not an integer (Lemma
3.5
). We do not keep track of the extra exponent
C_{2}
to avoid unnecessary heaviness, as the obtained exponent is very likely suboptimal.

As pointed out in remark 4 of
Tang and Yang 2023
, some authors consider a more general setting than ours, by allowing the observed data to contain noise. It usually consists in supposing that instead of observing data
X_{i}\sim\mu
i.i.d, we observe
Y_{i}=X_{i}+\epsilon_{i}
with
\epsilon_{i}
i.i.d. errors. In this setting,
Genovese et al. 2012
showed that when the error follows the standard Gaussian distribution, the minimax rate for estimating a manifold under the
Hausdorff distance is lower bounded by
O(\log(n)^{-1})
. To bypass this
slow convergence, previous work like
Divol 2022
, assume the noise variance
to decay with the number of data so that it does not impact the rate of convergence. In our setting, supposing we observe noisy data with
\|\epsilon_{1}\|\leq n^{-\frac{\beta+\gamma}{2\beta+d}}\vee n^{-\frac{1}{2}}
almost surely, will not impact Theorem
4.1
as the noise scales at the minimax rate.

Theorem
4.1
shows that the GAN estimator is minimax optimal (up to the logarithmic factor) for the distance
d_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\gamma}}
. Nevertheless, this result does not really translate to practical applications as the class of discriminators
\mathcal{D}
(
4.2
) we used is not tractable. Due to the lack of smoothness of the target measure
g_{\#U}^{\star}
, we had to use the fact that the class
\mathcal{G}
is small rather than it is of smoothness
\beta+1
. Supposing that the target measure has a smooth density with respect to the volume measure of a submanifold, we show in the next sections that we can exploit this regularity and therefore, use a tractable class of discriminators.

4.2
Model
2
: A tractable
\mathcal{D}
in full dimension

In this section we move away from the case of transport maps from the torus and treat the setting of Model
2
where the target measure
\mu
has a density
f^{\star}
with respect to the
p
-dimensional Lebesgue measure. The goal is to understand how to build a tractable estimator of densities on submanifolds, in the simple case where the submanifold is the whole
\mathbb{R}^{p}
space.

Let us first state an analog of Theorem
3.1
in the case of densities.

Corollary 4.2
.

If the target
f^{\star}
belongs to
\mathcal{H}^{\beta}_{K}(\mathbb{R}^{p},\mathbb{R})
, has support included in
B^{p}(0,K)
and
\mathcal{F}\subset\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{\infty,\infty}(B^{p}(0,K),\mathbb{R},K)
,
\mathcal{D}\subset\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{\infty,\infty}(B^{p}(0,K),\mathbb{R},1)
with
\gamma\in[1,\beta]
, then the density adversarial estimator (
2.2
) verifies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup\limits_{D\in\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}_{1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{p}}D(x)(\hat{f}(x)-f^{\star}(x))d\lambda^{p}(x)\Big]-\Delta_{\mathcal{F}}-\Delta_{\mathcal{D}}

\displaystyle\leq C\min\limits_{\delta\in[0,1]}\left\{\sqrt{\frac{(\delta+1/n)^{2}\log(n|\mathcal{F}_{1/n}||\mathcal{D}_{1/n}|)}{n}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}(1+\delta^{(1-\frac{p}{2\gamma})}+\log(\delta^{-1})\mathds{1}_{\{2\gamma=p\}})\right\}.

The proof of Corollary
4.2
can be found in Section
C.2.1
. Let us now build the classes of functions
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{D}
by adapting to the non periodic case, the construction of the class of generators (
4.1
) from the previous section. For
\eta\in(0,\beta+1)
and
\delta\in(0,1)
, define

\mathcal{F}^{\eta,\delta}=\{f\in\mathcal{B}^{\eta}_{\infty,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{p},\mathbb{R},C_{\eta}K)|\ \langle f,\psi_{jlw}\rangle_{L^{2}}=0,\forall j\geq\log_{2}(\delta^{-1})\text{ and }supp(f)\subset B^{p}(0,K)\}

the set of functions in
\mathcal{B}^{\eta}_{\infty,\infty}
that do not have high frequencies and have compact support in
B^{p}(0,K)
. As in Section
4.1
, we are going to approximate the class
\mathcal{F}^{\eta,\delta}
by neural networks. Let
\hat{\phi},\hat{\psi}
be the approximations by neural networks of the scaling and wavelet Daubechies function from (
A.4
) and (
A.5
) at precision
L^{-1}=n^{-1}
. For ease of notation
\hat{\phi},\hat{\psi}
will be written
\hat{\psi}_{0},\hat{\psi}_{1}
respectively and define the multi-dimensional approximation of the wavelet as

\hat{\psi}_{0w}(x)=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{p}\hat{\psi}_{0}(x_{i}-w_{i})\ \text{ , }\ \hat{\psi}_{jlw}(x)=2^{pj/2}\prod\limits_{i=1}^{p}\hat{\psi}_{l_{i}}(2^{j}x_{i}-w_{i}).

Using these approximated wavelets, Define the class of function
\hat{\mathcal{F}}
by

\hat{\mathcal{F}}^{\eta,\delta}:=\Big\{\sum\limits_{j=0}^{\log_{2}(\delta^{-1})}\sum\limits_{l=1}^{2^{p}}\sum\limits_{w\in\{-K2^{j},...,K2^{j}\}^{p}}\hat{\alpha}(j,l,w)\hat{\psi}_{jlw}\big|\ |\hat{\alpha}(j,l,w)|\leq C_{\eta}K2^{-j(\eta+p/2)}\Big\}.

(4.3)

As in the periodic case, we obtain that
\hat{\mathcal{F}}^{\eta,\delta}
and
\mathcal{F}^{\eta,\delta}
have the same bounds on their approximation errors and covering numbers.

Proposition 4.3
.

If
\|\phi-\hat{\phi}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{\lfloor\beta\rfloor+2}}\leq L^{-1}
with
L\geq\delta^{-(\lfloor\beta\rfloor+2+\gamma)}
, then

\Delta_{\hat{\mathcal{F}}^{\eta,\delta}}:=\sup\limits_{h^{\star}\in\mathcal{H}^{\eta}_{1}}\inf\limits_{f\in\hat{\mathcal{F}}^{\eta,\delta}}\sup\limits_{D\in\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}_{1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{p}}D(x)(f(x)-h^{\star}(x))d\lambda^{p}(x)\leq C\delta^{\eta+\gamma}

and
\forall\epsilon\in(L^{-1}\delta^{\eta-1},1)
,

\log(|\hat{\mathcal{F}}^{\eta,\delta}|_{\epsilon})\leq C\delta^{-p}\log(\lfloor\delta^{-1}\rfloor)\log(\epsilon^{-1}).

The proof of Proposition
4.3
can be found in Section
C.2.2
. Here, we need our class of generators to approximate at a scale
O(n^{-\frac{\beta+\gamma}{2\beta+p}})
the density
f^{\star}\in\mathcal{H}^{\beta}_{K}(\mathbb{R}^{p},\mathbb{R})
, while having a covering number that does not exceed
O(n^{\frac{p}{2\beta+p}})
(see (
3.4
)) up to logarithmic factors. Therefore, choosing
\eta=\beta
and
\delta_{n}=n^{-\frac{1}{2\beta+p}}
in Proposition
4.3
, we have

\inf\limits_{f\in\hat{\mathcal{F}}^{\beta,\delta_{n}}}\sup\limits_{D\in\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}_{1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{p}}D(x)(f(x)-f^{\star}(x))d\lambda^{p}(x)\leq Cn^{-\frac{\beta+\gamma}{2\beta+p}}

and

\log(|\left(\hat{\mathcal{F}}^{\beta,\delta_{n}}\right)_{1/n}|)\leq Cn^{\frac{p}{2\beta+p}}\log(n),

which makes
\hat{\mathcal{F}}^{\beta,\delta_{n}}
perfectly suited to be our class of generators.

We now develop a method that allows to exploit the smoothness of the target measure. We are going to define a class of discriminators
\mathcal{D}
such that the density adversarial estimator (
2.2
) attains minimax rates for all
d_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\gamma}}
,
\gamma\in[1,\beta]
simultaneously. Then, the question of what should be the regularity of the class of discriminators
\mathcal{D}
arises. Let us first show using Proposition
B.1
, that IPMs of high regularity control IPMs of lower regularity.

Corollary 4.4
.

Let be
f,f^{\star}\in\mathcal{H}^{\beta}_{K}(\mathbb{R}^{p},\mathbb{R})
compactly supported in
B^{p}(0,K)
. Then for all
\alpha\geq\gamma>0
, we have

d_{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma,2}_{\infty,\infty}(1)}(f,f^{\star})\leq Cd_{\mathcal{B}^{\alpha,2}_{\infty,\infty}(1)}(f,f^{\star})^{\frac{\beta+\gamma}{\beta+\alpha}}.

The proof of Corollary
4.4
can be found in Section
C.2.3
. This is the key result that will allow us to prove that if
\hat{f}
attains the minimax rate for
d_{\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}_{1}}
, then it will also attain the minimax rate for
d_{\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}_{1}}
for all
\gamma\in[1,\alpha]
. Indeed, by injection of Besov spaces into Holder spaces (up to logarithmic terms), Corollary
4.4
implies that for all
\epsilon\in(0,1)
,

d_{\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}_{1}}(f,f^{\star})\leq C\log(\epsilon^{-1})^{2}d_{\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}_{1}}(f,f^{\star})^{\frac{\beta+\gamma}{\beta+\alpha}}+\epsilon,

as detailed in Section
C.2.6
. Therefore, if for a certain
\alpha\in[1,\beta]
we obtain
d_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\alpha}}(\hat{f},f^{\star})\leq O(n^{-\frac{\beta+\alpha}{2\beta+p}})
, then
\forall\gamma\in[1,\alpha]
we will have up to logarithmic terms,

d_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\gamma}}(\hat{f},f^{\star})\leq Cd_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\alpha}}(\hat{f},f^{\star})^{\frac{\beta+\gamma}{\beta+\alpha}}\leq O(n^{-\frac{\beta+\alpha}{2\beta+p}})^{\frac{\beta+\gamma}{\beta+\alpha}}=O(n^{-\frac{\beta+\gamma}{2\beta+p}}).

(4.4)

Hence, by taking the regularity of the discriminator class to be equal to
\beta
, if we obtain
d_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\beta}}(\hat{f},f^{\star})\leq O(n^{-\frac{\beta+\beta}{2\beta+p}})
, we would have minimax rates for
d_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\gamma}}
for all
\gamma\in[1,\beta]
. However,
in the case where
\beta>p/2
the minimax rate for the distance
d_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\beta}}
is
O(n^{-1/2})
not
O(n^{-\frac{\beta+\beta}{2\beta+p}})
, so equation (
4.4
) does not work anymore. Therefore, we choose the regularity of the discriminator class
\mathcal{D}
to be

\tilde{\beta}:=\beta\wedge p/2.

At the end of the day, we choose

\mathcal{F}:=\hat{\mathcal{F}}^{\beta,n^{-\frac{1}{2\beta+p}}}\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathcal{D}:=\hat{\mathcal{F}}^{\tilde{\beta},n^{-\frac{1}{2\beta+p}}}

(4.5)

as classes of generators and discriminators.

As for Model 1,
\mathcal{F}
is a class of neural networks with one layer of
O(n^{d}\log(n))
blocks, each block with width
O(n)
and depth
O(1)
. Contrary to Model 1, the class of discriminator is now a class of neural networks, with the same dimensions as for
\mathcal{F}
.

We are first going to show that the density adversarial estimator (
2.2
) with these classes attains minimax rates for the distance
d_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\tilde{\beta}}}
. Then applying Corollary
4.4
, we will obtain that it also attains minimax rates for all
d_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\gamma}}
,
\gamma\in[1,\tilde{\beta}]
.

Now to obtain the rate for the distance
d_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\tilde{\beta}}}
, we need to bound the approximation error of the class
\mathcal{D}
which is defined in the density case as

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{D}}

\displaystyle:=\sup\limits_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\sup\limits_{D^{\star}\in\mathcal{H}^{\tilde{\beta}}_{1}}\inf\limits_{D\in\mathcal{D}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{p}}(D^{\star}(x)-D(x))(f(x)-f^{\star}(x))d\lambda^{p}(x).

Here, in contrast to the assumptions of Model
1
, the target measure
f^{\star}
has some regularity from the point of view of the
p
-dimensional Lebesgue measure. Therefore, as illustrated in the following lemma, we can give the
\beta
-regularity of the function
f-f^{\star}
to the generators
D^{\star}-D
and bound
\Delta_{\mathcal{D}}
by the approximation error of the
\tilde{\beta}+\beta
-smooth map
\Gamma^{-\beta}(D^{\star})
.

Lemma 4.5
.

For
\mathcal{D}
defined in (
4.5
) we have

\Delta_{\mathcal{D}}\leq C\sup\limits_{h\in\mathcal{B}^{0}_{\infty,\infty}}\sup\limits_{D^{\star}\in\mathcal{H}^{\tilde{\beta}}_{1}}\inf\limits_{D\in\mathcal{D}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{p}}(\Gamma^{-\beta}(D^{\star})(x)-\Gamma^{-\beta}(D)(x))h(x)d\lambda^{p}(x).

The proof of Lemma
4.5
can be found in Section
C.2.4
. Putting the bounds of Lemma
4.5
and Proposition
4.3
in Corollary
4.2
, we obtain that the density adversarial estimator is minimax for the distance
d_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\tilde{\beta}}}
.

Proposition 4.6
.

For
\hat{f}
the density adversarial estimator (
2.2
) using the classes
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{D}
defined in (
4.5
), we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[d_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\tilde{\beta}}}(\hat{f},f^{\star})\Big]\leq C\log(n)^{C_{2}}n^{-\frac{\beta+\tilde{\beta}}{2\beta+p}}.

The proof of Proposition
4.6
can be found in Section
C.2.5
. Using Corollary
4.4
, we can now show the final bound for the density adversarial estimator (
2.2
) in the setting of Model
2
.

Proposition 4.7
.

For all
f^{\star}\in\mathcal{H}^{\beta+1}_{K}(\mathbb{R}^{p},\mathbb{R})
, the density adversarial estimator
\hat{f}
of (
2.2
) using the classes
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{D}
defined in (
4.5
) verifies for all
\gamma\in[1,\beta]

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[d_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\gamma}}(\hat{f},f^{\star})\Big]\leq C\log(n)^{C_{2}}\left(n^{-\frac{\beta+\gamma}{2\beta+p}}\vee n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right).

The proof of Proposition
4.7
can be found in Section
C.2.6
. We can conclude that the density adversarial estimator attains minimax optimal rates up to logarithmic factors in the density case. Note that we had to distinguish whether
\beta\leq p/2
or not.

•

In the case
\beta<p/2
, the estimator
\hat{f}
is computed using a discriminator class of regularity
\beta
and attains minimax rates for all
\gamma\in[1,\beta]
at the same time.

•

In the case
\beta\geq p/2
, the estimator
\hat{f}
is computed using a discriminator class of regularity
p/2
and attains minimax rates for all
\gamma\in[1,\infty)
at the same time, as for
\gamma\geq p/2
,
d_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\gamma}}(\hat{f},f^{\star})\leq d_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{p/2}}(\hat{f},f^{\star})
.

As mentioned in Section
2.2
, the density adversarial estimator (
2.2
) does not provide a direct way of sampling from the approximate density
\hat{f}
. However, one could obtain such estimator by supposing that the target density
f^{\star}
is the push forward measure of a known easy to sample density
\nu
on
\mathbb{R}^{p}
. In this case, defining the GAN estimator as

\hat{g}\in\argmin\limits_{g\in\mathcal{G}}\sup\limits_{D\in\mathcal{D}}\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}D(X_{i})-D(g(Y_{i}))

for
Y_{i}\sim\nu
i.i.d. and
\mathcal{G}\subset\mathcal{H}^{\beta+1}_{K}(\mathbb{R}^{p},\mathbb{R}^{p})
, one could prove (following the same method developed above) that this estimator attains minimax rates of convergence. We do not develop further on this estimator as the main purpose of this paper is to provide results for the manifold case of Model
3
. Having developed a method for the full dimensional case, we adapt it in the following section to the case where the submanifold is of dimension
d<p
.

5
The manifold case

In this section, we suppose that we are in the setting of Model
3
.
Using this hypothesis we have that
g^{\star}_{\#U}
admits a density with respect to the volume measure of the manifold
\mathcal{M}_{g^{\star}}=g^{\star}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
and that its density
f_{g^{\star}}
belongs to
\mathcal{H}^{\beta}_{C}(\mathcal{M}_{g^{\star}},\mathbb{R})
. In order to adapt the method of the preceding section, we first prove an interpolation inequality similar to Corollary
4.4
in this setting. Then, we use it to prove minimax optimality of the GAN estimator.

5.1
Interpolation inequality for smooth densities on manifolds

In this section we want to prove an analog version of Corollary
4.4
but in the context of Model
3
. The difficulty comes from the fact that although our push forward maps
g,g^{\star}:\mathbb{T}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{p}
belong to
\mathcal{H}^{\beta+1}
, the densities they produce on their respective images do not have regularity from the point of view of the
p
-dimensional Lebesgue measure. We will see that in order to exploit the regularity of the push forward maps, we can split the cost
\mathbb{E}[D(g(U))-D(g^{\star}(U))]
into two terms: one characterizing the distance between the support of
g_{\#U}
and
g^{\star}_{\#U}
, and the other characterizing their difference in terms of density. Let us first state the desired result and then detail its proof.

Theorem 5.1
.

Let
g,g^{\star}\in\mathcal{H}^{\beta+1}_{K}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{p})
that verify the
K
-manifold regularity condition with
g^{\star}
that verifies the
K
-density regularity condition of Definition
2.1
. There exists constants
C,C_{2}>0
such that if

d_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\beta+1}}(g_{\#U},g^{\star}_{\#U})\leq C^{-1}
, then for all
\epsilon\in(0,1)
and
\gamma\in[1,\beta+1]
, we have

\displaystyle d_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\gamma}}(g_{\#U},g^{\star}_{\#U})\leq C_{2}\log(\epsilon^{-1})^{4}\left(d_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\beta+1}}(g_{\#U},g^{\star}_{\#U})^{\frac{\beta+\gamma}{2\beta+1}}+\epsilon\right).

Compared to Corollary
4.4
,
g_{\#U}
and
g_{\#U}^{\star}
need to be already close in
d_{\mathcal{H}^{\beta+1}_{1}}
distance for Theorem
5.1
to apply. This assumption will imply that there exists a diffeomorphism between their supports with several key properties (Proposition
D.5
). The precise definition of these properties is gathered into the notion of
(g_{\#U},g^{\star}_{\#U})
-compatible map (Definition
D.3
) and can be found in the appendix Section
D.1
. In a nutshell, for
t>d_{\mathcal{H}^{\beta+1}_{1}}(g_{\#U},g^{\star}_{\#U})
, a map
T:\mathcal{M}_{g^{\star}}^{t}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{g^{\star}}
being
(g_{\#U},g^{\star}_{\#U})
-compatible is a projection onto
\mathcal{M}_{g^{\star}}
such that its restriction to
\mathcal{M}_{g}
is a
C^{\beta+1}
-diffeomorphism.

Let us now prove Theorem
5.1
through
(g_{\#U},g^{\star}_{\#U})
-compatibility.
For
D\in\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{p},\mathbb{R})
and
T
being
(g_{\#U},g^{\star}_{\#U})
-compatible, write

\mathbb{E}_{U\sim\mathcal{U}([0,1]^{d})}\Big[D(g(U))-D(g^{\star}(U))\Big]=A_{1}+A_{2},

with

A_{1}:=\mathbb{E}_{U\sim\mathcal{U}([0,1]^{d})}\Big[D(g(U))-D(T(g(U)))\Big]

characterizing the distance between the supports, and

A_{2}:=\mathbb{E}_{U\sim\mathcal{U}([0,1]^{d})}\Big[D(T(g(U)))-D(g^{\star}(U))\Big]

characterizing the distance between the densities.
Splitting the cost this way allows to finely exploit the regularity of
g
and
g^{\star}
:

•

The term
A_{2}
writes as an integral of a product of functions defined on the same support
\mathcal{M}_{g^{\star}}
.
Indeed, denoting by
f_{T_{g}}
the density of
(T\circ g)_{\#U}
with respect to the volume measure
\lambda_{\mathcal{M}_{g^{\star}}}
, we have

A_{2}=\int_{\mathcal{M}_{g^{\star}}}D(x)(f_{T_{g}}(x)-f_{g^{\star}}(x))d\lambda_{\mathcal{M}_{g^{\star}}}(x).

Since the only support involved is
\mathcal{M}_{g^{\star}}
, wavelet-based expansions allow to transfer the regularity of the densities on
\mathcal{M}_{g^{\star}}
to the discriminator,
as in the the full-dimensional case (Corollary
4.4
).

•

The term
A_{1}
writes similarly, through the Taylor expansion

\displaystyle A_{1}=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{p}\int_{[0,1]^{d}}\int_{0}^{1}\partial_{i}D(g(u)+t(T(g(u))-g(u)))(T(g(u))-g(u))_{i}dtd\lambda^{d}(u).

Here, a similar wavelet-like expansion is made possible using the compatibility properties of
T:\mathcal{M}_{g}\to\mathcal{M}_{g^{\star}}
. However, extra technical difficulties arise to deal with the evaluation of a regularised version of
D
on the ambient points
\bigl(g(u)+t(T(g(u))-g(u)\bigr)_{0\leq t\leq 1}
, while the wavelet construction naturally takes place on the torus
\mathbb{T}^{d}
.

More formally, the bound on
A_{1}
comes from the following result.

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
g,g^{\star}\in\mathcal{H}^{\beta+1}_{K}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{p})
that verify the
K
-manifold regularity condition with
g^{\star}
that verifies the
K
-density regularity condition. There exist constants
C,C_{2}>0
such that, if
d_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\beta+1}}(g_{\#U},g^{\star}_{\#U})\leq C^{-1}
, then for all
\epsilon\in(0,1)
and
\gamma\in[1,\beta+1]
, we have

\displaystyle d_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\gamma}}((T\circ g)_{\#U},g_{\#U})\leq C_{2}\log(\epsilon^{-1})^{4}\left(\sup\limits_{D\in\mathcal{H}^{\beta+1}_{1},D\circ T=0}\mathbb{E}_{U\sim\mathcal{U}([0,1]^{d})}[D(g(U))]^{\frac{\beta+\gamma}{2\beta+1}}+\epsilon\right).

The proof of Lemma
5.2
can be found in Section
D.3
.
Note that the right hand side only depends on
g^{\star}
through the constraint
D\circ T=0
, i.e. that
D_{|\mathcal{M}_{g^{\star}}}=0
. As a result, this term is oblivious to the mass distribution of
g^{\star}_{\#U}
over
\mathcal{M}_{g^{\star}}
, and only measures distances between the support
\mathcal{M}_{g}
and
\mathcal{M}_{g^{\star}}
.

To deduce a bound on
A_{1}
, observe that

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{D\in\mathcal{H}^{\beta+1}_{1},D\circ T=0}\mathbb{E}_{U\sim\mathcal{U}([0,1]^{d})}[D(g(U))]

\displaystyle=\sup\limits_{D\in\mathcal{H}^{\beta+1}_{1},D\circ T=0}\mathbb{E}_{U\sim\mathcal{U}([0,1]^{d})}[D(g(U))-D(g^{\star}(U))]

\displaystyle\leq d_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\beta+1}}(g_{\#U},g^{\star}_{\#U}),

which finally leads to

A_{1}\leq C_{2}\log(\epsilon^{-1})^{4}\left(d_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\beta+1}}(g_{\#U},g^{\star}_{\#U})^{\frac{\beta+\gamma}{2\beta+1}}+\epsilon\right).

The term
A_{2}
may be bounded using the following result.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
g,g^{\star}\in\mathcal{H}^{\beta+1}_{K}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{p})
that verify the
K
-manifold regularity condition with
g^{\star}
that verifies the
K
-density regularity condition. There exist constants
C,C_{2}>0
such that if
d_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\beta+1}}(g_{\#U},g^{\star}_{\#U})\leq C^{-1},
then for all
\epsilon\in(0,1)
and
\gamma\in[1,\beta+1]
, we have

\displaystyle d_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\gamma}}((T\circ g)_{\#U},g^{\star}_{\#U})\leq C_{2}\log(\epsilon^{-1})\sup\limits_{D\in\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\beta+1}(\mathcal{M}_{g^{\star}},\mathbb{R})}\mathbb{E}_{U}[D(T(g(U)))-D(g^{\star}(U))]^{\frac{\beta+\gamma}{2\beta+1}}+\epsilon.

The proof of Lemma
5.3
can be found in Section
D.4
. For
D\in\mathcal{H}^{\beta+1}_{1}(\mathcal{M}_{g^{\star}},\mathbb{R})
, we have
D\circ T\in\mathcal{H}^{\beta+1}_{C}(\mathcal{M}_{g^{\star}}^{t},\mathbb{R})
. Thus, using Theorem
D.20
, we can show (like for Proposition
D.17
) that
D\circ T
can be extended globally to a map in
\mathcal{H}^{\beta+1}_{C}(\mathbb{R}^{p},\mathbb{R})
. In particular, we have

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{D\in\mathcal{H}^{\beta+1}_{1}(\mathcal{M}_{g^{\star}},\mathbb{R})}\mathbb{E}_{U\sim\mathcal{U}([0,1]^{d})}[D(T(g(U)))-D(g^{\star}(U))]

\displaystyle\leq Cd_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\beta+1}}(g_{\#U},g^{\star}_{\#U}).

Hence, from Lemma
5.3
we deduce that

A_{2}\leq C_{2}\log(\epsilon^{-1})d_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\beta+1}}(g_{\#U},g^{\star}_{\#U})^{\frac{\beta+\gamma}{2\beta+1}}+\epsilon,

that concludes the proof of Theorem
5.1
.

Just as in the full-dimensional case (Section
4.2
), the next section builds upon this interpolation bound to show that GAN estimators attain optimal estimation rates for all the distances
\bigl(d_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\gamma}}\bigr)_{\gamma\in[1,\beta+1]}
simultaneously.

5.2
A tractable GAN estimator in the manifold case

We are now in position to define our classes of generators and discriminators for the GAN estimator in the setting of Model
3
. We use the class of generators
\mathcal{G}
from Section
4.1
and the class of discriminators
\mathcal{D}
from Section
4.2
(both computable in practice). As in Section
4.2
, we first show that the GAN estimator attains minimax rates for the distance
d_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\tilde{\beta}+1}}
(for
\tilde{\beta}
defined in (
5.2
)). Then applying Theorem
5.1
, we obtain that it also attains minimax rates for all
d_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\gamma}}
,
\gamma\in[1,\beta+1]
.

In order to apply Theorem
5.1
to the GAN estimator
\hat{g}
, we need it to verify the manifold regularity condition of Definition
2.1
. We define in the Appendix Section
E.1
, the
\chi
-numerical regularity condition that implies the manifold regularity condition. In a nutshell, it is an easy to compute condition that ensures that if two points are far in the torus, then their images by
\hat{g}
are
not too close. We choose

\mathcal{G}:=\{g\in\hat{\mathcal{F}}^{\beta+1,n^{-\frac{1}{2\beta+d}}}_{per}|\ g\text{ verifies the }\chi\text{-numerical regularity condition}\}

(5.1)

to be the class of generators.

As mentioned in Section
4.1
,
the approximation error and covering numbers of the class
\hat{\mathcal{F}}^{\beta+1,n^{-\frac{1}{2\beta+d}}}_{per}
are well suited for the GAN to attain minimax rates (see Proposition
3.7
). Proposition
E.5
shows that requiring the generators to verify the
\chi
-numerical regularity condition does not deteriorate the approximation error of the class
\mathcal{G}
. Define

\tilde{\beta}+1:=(\beta+1)\wedge d/2,

(5.2)

write
\tilde{\delta}_{n}:=n^{-\frac{1}{2\tilde{\beta}+d}}
, and choose

\mathcal{D}:=\hat{\mathcal{F}}^{\tilde{\beta}+1,\tilde{\delta}_{n}}

(5.3)

to be the class of discriminators.

Up to the
\chi
-numerical regularity condition (that may be practically enforced using a regularization term, see the remark below Proposition
E.2
),
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{D}
are classes of neural networks with the same structure as in Model 2.

The specific discriminator regularity
\tilde{\beta}+1
comes naturally in light of Theorem
5.1
. Using the same argument as above (
4.4
) for the full-dimensional case,
\tilde{\beta}+1
is a regularity cap for the discriminator class, over which no gain may be expected in terms of convergence of the GAN (recall that the convergence rate in terms of
d_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\gamma}}
cannot be faster than
n^{-1/2}
).

However, the use of this discriminator class raises two problems.

•

The logarithm of its
1/n
-covering is of order
O(n^{\frac{p}{2\tilde{\beta}+d}})
, which is too large as it depends on the ambient dimension
p
.

•

It only approximates
\mathcal{H}^{\tilde{\beta}+1}_{1}
up to an error
O(n^{-\frac{\tilde{\beta}+1}{2\tilde{\beta}+d}})
in sup norm, so it is not clear whether we can obtain that
\Delta_{\mathcal{D}}
is of order
O(n^{-\frac{2\tilde{\beta}+1}{2\tilde{\beta}+d}})
.

To bypass these problems, we sharpen the bound given by Theorem
3.1
through a finer analysis of the bias and variance terms of the class
\mathcal{D}
. The quantities
\log(|\mathcal{D}_{1/n}|)
and
\Delta_{\mathcal{D}}
will be replaced using the following
finer (but more involved) quantities.

•

For
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, let

|(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{G}})_{\epsilon}|:=\sup_{g\in\mathcal{G}}\left|\left(\{D\circ g\ |\ D\in\mathcal{D}\}\right)_{\epsilon}\right|,

be the largest
\epsilon
-covering number of the class
\{D\circ g\ |\ D\in\mathcal{D}\}
among
g\in\mathcal{G}
.

•

For
g\in\mathcal{G}
, define

D_{g}^{\star}\in\argmax\limits_{D\in\mathcal{B}^{\tilde{\beta}+1}_{\infty,\infty}(C)}\mathbb{E}[D(g(U))-D(g^{\star}(U))]\quad\text{ and }\quad\overline{D}_{g}\in\argmin\limits_{D\in\mathcal{D}}\|D-D_{g}^{\star}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{0}_{\infty,\infty}},

as the optimal discriminator within
\mathcal{B}^{\tilde{\beta}+1}_{\infty,\infty}
and its approximation by
\mathcal{D}
.
Let us write

\Delta_{\mathcal{D}}^{g}:=\mathbb{E}[D_{g}^{\star}(g(U))-D_{g}^{\star}(g^{\star}(U))-(\overline{D}_{g}(g(U))-\overline{D}_{g}(g^{\star}(U)))]

for the discrimination error between
g_{\#U}
and
g^{\star}_{\#U}
of the class
\mathcal{D}
compared to the class
\mathcal{B}^{\tilde{\beta}+1}_{\infty,\infty}(C)
.

Using these two quantities, Theorem
3.1
can be sharpened as follows.

Theorem 5.4
.

For all
g^{\star}\in\mathcal{H}^{\beta+1}_{K}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{p})
verifying the
K
-manifold and
K
-density regularity conditions of Definition
2.1
, the GAN estimator
\hat{g}
of (
1.3
), with
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{D}
defined in (
5.1
) and (
5.3
), satisfies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[d_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\tilde{\beta}+1}}(\hat{g}_{\#U},g^{\star}_{\#U})\Big]-\Delta_{\mathcal{G}}-\mathbb{E}[\Delta^{\hat{g}}_{\mathcal{D}}]\leq

\displaystyle C\min\limits_{\delta\in[0,1]}\Biggl\{\sqrt{\frac{(\delta+1/n)^{2}\log(n|\mathcal{G}_{1/n}||(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{G}})_{1/n}|)}{n}}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}(1+\delta^{(1-\frac{d}{2(\tilde{\beta}+1)})}+\log(\delta^{-1})\mathds{1}_{\{\beta+1=d/2\}})\Biggl\}.

The proof of Theorem
5.4
can be found in Section
E.3
. Contrary to
\Delta_{\mathcal{D}}
, the term
\mathbb{E}[\Delta^{\hat{g}}_{\mathcal{D}}]
depends only on the approximation error of
\mathcal{D}
for the candidate minimizer
\hat{g}
. On the other hand, in contrast to
\log(|\mathcal{D}_{1/n}|)
, the term
\log(|(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{G}})_{1/n}|)
does not depend on the dimension of the ambient space
p
as shown in the following result.

Lemma 5.5
.

For
g\in\text{Lip}_{K}(\mathbb{T}^{d},[0,1]^{p})
, there exists an
\epsilon
-covering
(\mathcal{N}_{g})_{\epsilon}
of the class
\mathcal{N}_{g}=\{D\circ g\ |\ D\in\hat{\mathcal{F}}^{\tilde{\beta}+1,\delta}\}
, such that

\log(|(\mathcal{N}_{g})_{\epsilon}|)\leq C\delta^{-d}\log(\delta^{-1})\log(\epsilon^{-1}).

The proof of Lemma
5.5
can be found in Section
E.4
.
For
\delta=\tilde{\delta}_{n}=n^{-\frac{1}{2\tilde{\beta}+d}}
, we deduce that
\log(|(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{G}})_{1/n}|)\leq C\log(n)^{2}n^{\frac{d}{2\tilde{\beta}+d}}
, so it scales roughly like
\log(|\mathcal{G}_{1/n}|)
.

Let us now explain how to bound the term
\mathbb{E}[\Delta^{\hat{g}}_{\mathcal{D}}]
. Compared to Model
1
,
g^{\star}_{\#U}
has some regularity with respect to a smooth submanifold. Therefore, this regularity can be exploited with Theorem
5.1
in the following manner.

Lemma 5.6
.

There exists a constant
C>0
such that, if
g\in\mathcal{G}
verifies the
\chi
-manifold regularity condition and

d_{\mathcal{H}^{\tilde{\beta}+1}}(g_{\#U},g_{\#U}^{\star})\leq C^{-1},

then we have

\Delta_{\mathcal{D}}^{g}\leq C_{2}\log(n)^{C_{3}}\|\nabla D_{g}^{\star}-\nabla\overline{D}_{g}\|_{\infty}\Big(d_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\tilde{\beta}+1}}(g_{\#U},g^{\star}_{\#U})^{\frac{\tilde{\beta}+1}{2\tilde{\beta}+1}}+\frac{1}{n}\Big).

The proof of Lemma
5.6
can be found in Section
E.5
.
It shows that
\mathbb{E}[\Delta^{\hat{g}}_{\mathcal{D}}]
depends both on the precision of the approximation of
\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\tilde{\beta}+1}
by
\mathcal{D}
, and on the
d_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\tilde{\beta}+1}}
distance between
\hat{g}_{\#U}
and
g^{\star}_{\#U}
.
Therefore, if
\hat{g}_{\#U}
is close to
g^{\star}_{\#U}
, the term
\mathbb{E}[\Delta^{\hat{g}}_{\mathcal{D}}]
is much smaller than
\Delta_{\mathcal{D}}
.

From Lemmas
5.5
and
5.6
, we obtain that the GAN estimator built with the neural network classes
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{D}
defined in (
5.1
) and (
5.3
) is minimax for the distance
d_{\mathcal{H}^{\tilde{\beta}+1}}
.

Theorem 5.7
.

For all
g^{\star}\in\mathcal{H}^{\beta+1}_{K}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{p})
verifying the
K
-manifold and
K
-density regularity conditions of Definition
2.1
, the GAN estimator
\hat{g}
of (
1.3
), with
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{D}
defined in (
5.1
) and (
5.3
), satisfies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[d_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\tilde{\beta}+1}}(\hat{g}_{\#U},g^{\star}_{\#U})\Big]\leq C\log(n)^{C_{2}}n^{-\frac{2\tilde{\beta}+1}{2\tilde{\beta}+d}}.

The proof of Theorem
5.7
can be found in Section
E.6
.
Finally, combining Theorem
5.7
with the interpolation bound of Theorem
5.1
, the main result follows: over Model
3
, GAN estimators attain minimax optimal rates for all the distances
\bigl(d_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\gamma}}\bigr)_{\gamma\in[1,\beta+1]}
simultaneously.

Theorem 5.8
.

For all
g^{\star}\in\mathcal{H}^{\beta+1}_{K}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{p})
verifying the
K
-manifold and
K
-density regularity conditions of Definition
2.1
, the GAN estimator
\hat{g}
(
1.3
), with
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{D}
defined in (
5.1
) and (
5.3
), satisfies, for all
\gamma\in[1,\tilde{\beta}+1]
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[d_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\gamma}}(\hat{g}_{\#U},g^{\star}_{\#U})\Big]\leq C\log(n)^{C_{2}}\left(n^{-\frac{\beta+\gamma}{2\beta+d}}\vee n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right).

As in the full dimensional case, we have:

•

When
\beta+1<d/2
,
\hat{g}
is computed using a discriminator class of regularity
\beta+1
and attains minimax rates up to logarithmic factors for all
\gamma\in[1,\beta+1]
at the same time.

•

When
\beta+1\geq d/2
,
\hat{g}
is computed using a discriminator class of regularity
d/2
and attains minimax rates up to logarithmic factors for all
\gamma\in[1,\infty)
at the same time.

To the best of our knowledge, the GAN estimator (
1.3
) is the first tractable estimator attaining minimax rates up to logarithmic factors in the manifold setting. It is also the first one to be proved to be minimax for several IPMs simultaneously. On the other hand, the fact that we attain minimax rates shows in particular that Theorem
5.1
is sharp up to the logarithmic factors.

In
Liang 2021
, the author shows that in the case
\gamma<d/2
, if we take
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}_{1}
as the discriminator class for the GAN estimator (
1.3
), then the rate of error is
O(n^{-\frac{\gamma}{d}})
which is strictly slower than the minimax. This is due to the fact that the class
\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}_{1}
is so massive that it is able to well discriminate between empirical and smooth measures. Therefore, it will be more sensitive to the distance between the data itself than the underlying measures behind it. This is well illustrated by the fact that for any measure
\nu
with a smooth density with respect to a closed
d
-dimensional manifold
\mathcal{M}\subset\mathbb{R}^{p}
, we have (Lemma 13 in
Tang and Yang 2023
)

C_{1}\log(n)^{-1}n^{-\frac{\gamma}{d}}\leq\mathbb{E}_{X_{i},Y_{i}\sim\nu}\Big[d_{\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}_{1}}(\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{X_{i}},\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{Y_{i}})\Big]\leq C_{2}n^{-\frac{\gamma}{d}}.

Although the
X_{i}
’s and
Y_{i}
’s are i.i.d. with the same distribution, the
d_{\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}_{1}}
distance between their empirical distribution is strictly greater than the minimax rate. To overcome this problem,
Liang 2021
and
Tang and Yang 2023
use as estimator

\hat{\mu}\in\argmin\limits_{\nu\in S}\sup_{D\in\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}_{1}}\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[D(X)]-\hat{\mathcal{J}}(D),

where
\hat{\mathcal{J}}
a regularization of the integration of
D
under the empirical measure
\mu_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{X_{i}}
. Theorem
5.8
shows that actually, it is not necessary to regularize
\mu_{n}
to obtain minimax rates. Taking a discriminator class
\mathcal{D}
that is much smaller than the Hölder functions suffices to
implicitly regularize the measures, as this class is not able to distinguish between discrete and smooth measures.
This result gives a theoretical explanation of the well known fact that WGANs work because the discriminator class fails to approximate the Wasserstein distance as studied in
Stanczuk et al. 2021
.

Discussion

In this paper, we provided minimax rates of convergence of the GAN estimator in a general setting. This is a step towards a theoretical understanding of why Generative Adversarial Networks obtain state of the art results in various fields. On the other hand,
it shows that it is not necessary to regularize the empirical measure in the GAN estimator (
1.3
) (like in
Liang 2021
and
Tang and Yang 2023
) to obtain minimax rates.

Our analysis suggests that if the target measure has some higher Hölder regularity than the class used in the IPM, one should use discriminators having the same regularity than the target. This is supported by our inequality between the discrimination of different regularities (Theorem
5.1
). It shows that the Hölder class of the same regularity than the target distinguishes just as efficiently as the classes of weaker regularity (in particular the Wasserstein).

Our results strongly highlight the use of wavelets to describe function’s regularity. From a theoretical point of view, wavelets are only suitable tools to get interpolation inequalities such as Theorem
5.1
. On a practical side, they allow to easily enforce regularities of both generators and discriminators.
Provided that such high-order regularity constraints can be made tractable on general neural networks, other architectures than the ones we propose in Sections
4
and
5
might be proved optimal.
On the other hand, any class of functions able to approximate Daubechies wavelets well would work.
Nonetheless, approximating Daubechies wavelets is not an easy task (see, e.g.,
Daubechies and Lagarias 1991
), a using neural networks for this purpose seems to be a relevant strategy (see, e.g.,
Daubechies et al. 2023
).

Overall, this suggests that a "Generative Adversarial Wavelet" estimator would be perfectly suited to estimate smooth densities on arbitrary manifolds, and that using neural networks may be a relevant way to implement it. Future research will be focused on extending our analysis of this type of estimator and on numerical experiments to see how it performs compared to the state of the art. Furthermore, we could also consider moving away from the classical GAN setting with push-forward measures and extend our results to the case of manifolds with several charts.

Supplement to "Wasserstein Generative Adversarial Networks are Minimax Optimal Distribution Estimators"

The supplement contains auxiliary results and the neural network construction, together with all the proofs.

Contents

A
Additional notation and Neural Network construction

In this section, we define some additional notation needed for the poofs and we detail the construction of the Neural Networks used by our classes of generators and discriminators.

A.1
Additional notation

We will write
\text{Lip}_{1}
the set of 1-Lipschitz functions. The support of a function
f
will be denoted by
supp(f)
. For a path
\gamma:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{T}^{d}
, we write
\dot{\gamma}
its differential. For
u,v\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
in the
d
-dimensional torus, we write
\|u-v\|=\min\{\int_{0}^{1}\|\dot{\gamma}(t)\|dt\ |\ \gamma:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{T}^{d},\gamma(0)=u,\gamma(1)=v\}
as the periodised Euclidean norm. The
d
-dimensional ball of radius
\epsilon>0
in the torus for this distance will be written
B^{d}(0,\epsilon)
.
The Hausdorff distance between two subset
A,B\subset\mathbb{R}^{p}
will be denoted
\mathbb{H}(A,B)
. For
x
a point belonging either to
\mathbb{R}^{p},\mathbb{T}^{d}
or
\mathbb{R}
, we will write
O(x)
to denote a quantity that is bounded by
C\|x\|
for
C>0
independent of
x
.

To lighten the notation, for a function
f:\mathbb{R}^{p}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
that admits a wavelet expansion in
L^{2}
:

f(x)=\sum\limits_{w\in\mathbb{Z}^{p}}\alpha_{f}(w)\psi_{0w}(x)+\sum\limits_{j=0}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{l=1}^{2^{p}-1}\sum\limits_{w\in\mathbb{Z}^{p}}\alpha_{f}(j,l,w)\psi_{jlw}(x),

we will write

f(x)=\sum\limits_{j=0}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{l=1}^{2^{p}}\sum\limits_{w\in\mathbb{Z}^{p}}\alpha_{f}(j,l,w)\psi_{jlw}(x),

with the convention that
\psi_{02^{p}w}=\psi_{0w}
and for all
j\geq 1
,
\psi_{j2^{p}w}=0
. Likewise, the wavelet expansion of a map
f:\mathbb{T}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
will be written

f(u)=\sum\limits_{j=0}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{l=1}^{2^{d}}\sum\limits_{z\in\{0,...,2^{j}-1\}^{d}}\alpha_{f}(j,l,w)\psi_{jlz}^{per}(u),

with the convention that
\psi_{02^{d}0}=1
and for all
z\neq 0
,
j\geq 1
,
\psi^{per}_{02^{d}z}=\psi^{per}_{j2^{d}z}=\psi^{per}_{j2^{d}0}=0
.

Recalling the definition of Besov spaces from Section
3.2
, in the case
q_{1}=q_{1}=\infty
, the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{B}^{s,b}_{q_{1},q_{2}}}
of a function
f:\mathbb{R}^{p}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
value

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}^{s,b}_{\infty,\infty}}=

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{j\geq 0}2^{j(s+p/2)}(1+j)^{b}\sum\limits_{l=1}^{2^{p}}\sup\limits_{w\in\mathbb{Z}^{p}}|\alpha_{f}(j,l,w)|.

Likewise, for a function
f:\mathbb{T}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
we have

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}^{s,b}_{\infty,\infty}}=

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{j\geq 0}2^{j(s+p/2)}(1+j)^{b}\sum\limits_{l=1}^{2^{d}}\sup\limits_{z\in\{0,...,2^{j}-1\}^{d}}|\alpha_{f}(j,l,z)|.

A.2
Approximation of low wavelet frequencies by Neural Networks

A.2.1
Theoretical construction of the approximated wavelet

Let us describe how to approximate the class
\mathcal{F}^{\eta,\delta}_{per}
of (
3.8
) by neural networks. The first ingredient we need, is to have a good approximation
\hat{\phi}
of the Daubechies scaling function
\phi\in\mathcal{H}^{\lfloor\beta\rfloor+3}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R})

(
Daubechies 1988
)
, that will then give us a good approximation of the wavelet function

\psi=\sum\limits_{k=-\infty}^{+\infty}\lambda_{k}\phi(2\cdot-k).

(A.1)

The classical technique to approximate
\phi
is to use that it is solution to the two-scale difference equation
(
Daubechies and Lagarias 1991
)
:

\phi(x)=\sum\limits_{k=-\infty}^{+\infty}h_{k}\phi(2x-k)

(A.2)

for
h_{k}=2\int\phi(y)\phi(2y-k)dy
. From Theorem 4.2.10 in
(
Giné and Nickl 2015
)
, there exists
\phi
with compact support in
[0,N]
that belongs to
\mathcal{H}^{\lfloor\beta\rfloor+3}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R})
with
N\leq 12(\lfloor\beta\rfloor+3)
. Then (
A.2
) can be rewritten as

\phi(x)=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{N}h_{k}\phi(2x-k).

(A.3)

Using this property, the classical methods to approximate
\phi
are iterative schemes as the "cascade algorithm"
(
Daubechies and Lagarias 1991
)
. Here we use neural networks in order to connect with the "Network" part of the GAN. Any neural network architecture that allows to approximate
\phi
and its derivatives to an arbitrary precision would work. For example we can use the tanh neural
networks construction of
De Ryck et al. 2021
.

Lemma A.1
(Theorem 5.1,
De Ryck et al. 2021
).

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
L>0
, there exists a class of neural networks
\mathcal{T}_{L}^{k}\subset C^{\infty}([0,1]^{d},\mathbb{R}^{p})
with tanh activation function that is made of two hidden layers of width
CL
and
CL^{d}
respectively, such that for any
f\in\mathcal{H}_{1}^{k}([0,1]^{d},B^{p}(0,1))
, there exists a neural network
h_{f}\in\mathcal{T}_{L}^{k}
such that
\forall l\in\{0,...,k-1\}
we have

\|f-h_{f}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{l}}\leq C\frac{1}{L^{k-l}}.

The same way, we can also use the ReQU construction of
(
Belomestny et al. 2023
)
.

Lemma A.2
(Theorem 2,
Belomestny et al. 2023
).

For all
\alpha>2
and
L>0
, there exists a class of neural networks
\mathcal{R}_{L}^{\alpha}\subset\mathcal{H}^{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor}([0,1]^{d},\mathbb{R}^{p})
with ReQu activation function that is of width
CL^{d}
and depth
C
such that for any
f\in\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\alpha}([0,1]^{d},B^{p}(0,1))
, there exists a neural network
h_{f}\in\mathcal{R}_{L}^{\alpha}
such that
\forall l\in\{0,...,\lfloor\alpha\rfloor\}
we have

\|f-h_{f}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{l}}\leq C\frac{1}{L^{\alpha-l}}.

In
Puchkin et al. 2024
a bound on the covering number of the neural network class
\mathcal{R}_{L}^{\alpha}
is also provided. We do not use it as it depends on the number of parameters of the network which is to large here to obtain minimax rates. We instead give a smaller covering using the wavelet decomposition of the functions (Proposition
3.7
).

Fix
L>0
and define
\hat{\phi}\in\mathcal{T}_{L}^{\lfloor\beta\rfloor+2}
(or
\hat{\phi}\in\mathcal{R}_{L}^{\lfloor\beta\rfloor+2}
if we use ReQU instead of tanh) as the neural network from Lemma
A.1
(or Lemma
A.2
in the ReQU case) such that

\|\phi-\hat{\phi}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{\lfloor\beta\rfloor+2}}\leq CL^{-1}.

(A.4)

Recalling (
A.1
), define also

\hat{\psi}=\sum\limits_{k=-N+1}^{1}\lambda_{k}\hat{\phi}(2\cdot-k),

(A.5)

with
\lambda_{k}=(-1)^{k+1}h_{1-k}
for the approximation by neural networks of the wavelet function. Using these neural networks, let us approximate the periodised wavelets from (
3.7
).
Recalling that
\phi
has support in
[0,N]
and
\psi
in
[-N/2+1/2,N/2+1/2]
, then for
s\in[0,1]
and
z\ \in\{0,...,2^{j}-1\}
we have

\phi^{per}_{j}(s-2^{-j}z)=2^{j/2}\sum\limits_{k=\lceil-1-2^{-j}N\rceil}^{0}\phi(2^{j}(s-k)-z)

(A.6)

and

\psi^{per}_{j}(s-2^{-j}z)=2^{j/2}\sum\limits_{k=\lceil-1-2^{-(j+1)}(N+1)\rceil}^{\lfloor 1+2^{-(j+1)}(N-1)\rfloor}\psi(2^{j}(s-k)-z).

(A.7)

Note that if
2^{j}>N
then

\phi^{per}_{j}(s-2^{-j}z)=\phi(2^{j}s-z)+\phi(2^{j}(s+1)-z)

(A.8)

and

\psi^{per}_{j}(s-2^{-j}z)=\psi(2^{j}(s-1)-z)+\psi(2^{j}s-z)+\psi(2^{j}(s+1)-z).

(A.9)

Define the periodised wavelet approximations

\hat{\phi}^{per}_{j}(s)=2^{j/2}\sum\limits_{k=\lceil-1-2^{-j}N\rceil}^{0}\hat{\phi}(2^{j}(s-k))

and

\hat{\psi}^{per}_{j}(s)=2^{j/2}\sum\limits_{k=\lceil-1-2^{-(j+1)}(N+1)\rceil}^{\lfloor 1+2^{-(j+1)}(N-1)\rfloor}\hat{\psi}(2^{j}(s-k)).

for
\hat{\phi}
and
\hat{\psi}
from (
A.4
) and (
A.5
) . For ease of notation
\hat{\phi}^{per}
and
\hat{\psi}_{j}^{per}
will be written
\hat{\psi}^{per}_{j0},\hat{\psi}^{per}_{j1}
respectively. We finally define the multi-dimension approximation of the wavelet as

\hat{\psi}^{per}_{02^{d}0}(u)=1\ \text{ , }\ \hat{\psi}^{per}_{jlz}(u)=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{d}\hat{\psi}^{per}_{jl_{i}}(u_{i}-2^{-j}z_{i})\ l=1,...,2^{d},\ z\in\{0,...,2^{j}-1\}^{d}.

(A.10)

We use the convention that for all
z\neq 0
,
j\geq 1
,
\hat{\psi}^{per}_{02^{d}z}=\hat{\psi}^{per}_{j2^{d}z}=\hat{\psi}^{per}_{j2^{d}0}=0
. From Lemma 1 in
Belomestny et al. 2023
, the product
(x_{1},...,x_{d})\mapsto\prod_{i=1}^{d}x_{i}
can be exactly implemented by a REQU neural network with finite width and depth. On the other hand, from Corollary 3.7 in
De Ryck et al. 2021
, for all
\epsilon>0
there exists a one layer tanh neural network with finite width that implements the product
(x_{1},...,x_{d})\mapsto\prod_{i=1}^{d}x_{i}
up to an error
\epsilon
.

Now taking a function
f=\sum\limits_{j=0}^{\log_{2}(\delta^{-1})}\sum\limits_{l=1}^{2^{d}}\sum\limits_{z\in\{0,...,2^{j}-1\}^{d}}\hat{\alpha}_{f}(j,l,z)\hat{\psi}_{jlz}^{per}
, the next proposition states that the true wavelet coefficients
\alpha_{f}(j,l,z)
are close to its approximate coefficients
\hat{\alpha}_{f}(j,l,z)
.

Proposition A.3
.

For any
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathbb{R})
, if there exists a sequence
(\hat{\alpha}_{f}(j,l,z))
with
|\hat{\alpha}_{f}(j,l,z)|\leq C2^{-j(\eta+d/2)}
such that

f=\sum\limits_{j=0}^{\log_{2}(\delta^{-1})}\sum\limits_{l=1}^{2^{d}}\sum\limits_{z\in\{0,...,2^{j}-1\}^{d}}\hat{\alpha}_{f}(j,l,z)\hat{\psi}_{jlz}^{per},

then
\forall j\leq\log_{2}(\delta^{-1})
we have

|\alpha_{f}(j,l,z)-\hat{\alpha}_{f}(j,l,z)|\leq CL^{-1}\big(2^{-j(\lfloor\beta\rfloor+2+d/2)}\delta^{-(\lfloor\beta\rfloor+2-\eta)}\log_{2}(\delta^{-1})+2^{-j(\eta+d/2)}\big)

and
\forall j>\log_{2}(\delta^{-1})
we have

|\alpha_{f}(j,l,z)|\leq CL^{-1}2^{-j(\lfloor\beta\rfloor+2+d/2)}\delta^{-(\lfloor\beta\rfloor+2-\eta)}\log_{2}(\delta^{-1}).

The proof of Proposition
A.3
follows the same arguments than the proof of the following proposition.

Proposition A.4
.

For any
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{p},\mathbb{R})
, if there exists a sequence
(\hat{\alpha}_{f}(j,l,w))
with
|\hat{\alpha}_{f}(j,l,w)|\leq C2^{-j(\eta+p/2)}
such that

f=\sum\limits_{j=0}^{\log_{2}(\delta^{-1})}\sum\limits_{l=1}^{2^{p}}\sum\limits_{w\in\{-K2^{j},...,K2^{j}\}^{p}}\hat{\alpha}_{f}(j,l,w)\hat{\psi}_{jlw},

then
\forall j\leq\log_{2}(\delta^{-1})
we have

|\alpha_{f}(j,l,w)-\hat{\alpha}_{f}(j,l,w)|\leq CL^{-1}(2^{-j(\lfloor\beta\rfloor+2+p/2)}\delta^{-(\lfloor\beta\rfloor+2-\eta)}\log_{2}(\delta^{-1})+2^{-j(\eta+p/2)})

and
\forall j>\log_{2}(\delta^{-1})
we have

|\alpha_{f}(j,l,w)|\leq CL^{-1}2^{-j(\lfloor\beta\rfloor+2+p/2)}\delta^{-(\lfloor\beta\rfloor+2-\eta)}\log_{2}(\delta^{-1}).

Let
(j,l,w)\in\{1,...,\log(\delta^{-1})\}\times\{1,...,2^{p}\}\times\{-K2^{j},...,K2^{j}\}^{p}
, we have

\displaystyle\|\hat{\psi}_{jlw}-\psi_{jlw}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{\lfloor\beta\rfloor+2}}

\displaystyle\geq\|\hat{\psi}_{jlw}-\psi_{jlw}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\lfloor\beta\rfloor+2}_{\infty,\infty}}

\displaystyle=\sup\limits_{(j^{{}^{\prime}},l^{{}^{\prime}},w^{{}^{\prime}})}2^{j^{{}^{\prime}}(\lfloor\beta\rfloor+2+p/2)}|\alpha_{\hat{\psi}_{jlw}}(j^{{}^{\prime}},l^{{}^{\prime}},w^{{}^{\prime}})-\mathds{1}_{\{(j^{{}^{\prime}},l^{{}^{\prime}},w^{{}^{\prime}})=(j,l,w)\}}|.

Furthermore

\displaystyle\|

\displaystyle\hat{\psi}_{jlw}-\psi_{jlw}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{\lfloor\beta\rfloor+2}}

\displaystyle=2^{jp/2}\|\prod\limits_{i=1}^{p}\hat{\psi}_{l_{i}}(2^{j}\cdot_{i}-w_{i})-\prod\limits_{i=1}^{p}\psi_{l_{i}}(2^{j}\cdot_{i}-w_{i})\|_{\mathcal{H}^{\lfloor\beta\rfloor+2}}

\displaystyle=2^{jp/2}\|\sum\limits_{i=1}^{p}(\hat{\psi}_{l_{i}}(2^{j}\cdot_{i}-w_{i})-\psi_{l_{i}}(2^{j}\cdot_{i}-w_{i}))\prod\limits_{r>i}^{p}\hat{\psi}_{l_{r}}(2^{j}\cdot_{r}-w_{r})\prod\limits_{s<i}^{p}\psi_{l_{s}}(2^{j}\cdot_{s}-w_{s})\|_{\mathcal{H}^{\lfloor\beta\rfloor+2}}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{j(\lfloor\beta\rfloor+2+p/2)}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{p}\|(\hat{\psi}_{l_{i}}(\cdot_{i}-w_{i})-\psi_{l_{i}}(\cdot_{i}-w_{i}))\prod\limits_{r>i}^{p}\hat{\psi}_{l_{r}}(\cdot_{r}-w_{r})\prod\limits_{s<i}^{p}\psi_{l_{s}}(\cdot_{s}-w_{s})\|_{\mathcal{H}^{\lfloor\beta\rfloor+2}}

\displaystyle\leq C2^{j(\lfloor\beta\rfloor+2+p/2)}L^{-1},

recalling that
\|\hat{\psi}_{l_{i}}-\psi_{l_{i}}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{\lfloor\beta\rfloor+2}}\leq CL^{-1}
for
l_{i}\in\{0,1\}
.
We deduce that for all
(j^{{}^{\prime}},l^{{}^{\prime}},w^{{}^{\prime}})\neq(j,l,w)
we have

|\alpha_{\hat{\psi}_{jlw}}(j^{{}^{\prime}},l^{{}^{\prime}},w^{{}^{\prime}})|\leq C2^{(j-j^{{}^{\prime}})(\lfloor\beta\rfloor+2+p/2)}L^{-1}

and

|\alpha_{\hat{\psi}_{jlw}}(j,l,w)-1|\leq CL^{-1}.

Let
(j^{{}^{\prime}},l^{{}^{\prime}},w^{{}^{\prime}})\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{\star}\times\{1,...,2^{p}\}\times\mathbb{Z}^{p}
, as

supp(\hat{\psi}_{jlw}),supp(\psi_{jlw})\subset\bigotimes\limits_{i=1}^{p}[2^{-j}(w_{i}-N),2^{-j}(w_{i}+N)],

we have that if

\bigotimes\limits_{i=1}^{p}[2^{-j}(w_{i}-N),2^{-j}(w_{i}+N)]\bigcap\bigotimes\limits_{i=1}^{p}[2^{-j^{{}^{\prime}}}(w_{i}^{{}^{\prime}}-N),2^{-j^{{}^{\prime}}}(w_{i}^{{}^{\prime}}+N)]=\varnothing,

then

\langle\hat{\psi}_{jlw},\psi_{j^{{}^{\prime}}l^{{}^{\prime}}w^{{}^{\prime}}}\rangle=0.

Let

f=\sum\limits_{j=0}^{\log_{2}(\delta^{-1})}\sum\limits_{l=1}^{2^{p}}\sum\limits_{w\in\{-K2^{j},...,K2^{j}\}^{p}}\hat{\alpha}_{f}(j,l,w)\hat{\psi}_{jlw}\in\hat{\mathcal{F}}^{\eta,\delta},

taking
\hat{\alpha}_{f}(j,l,w):=0
for
j\geq\log_{2}(\delta^{-1})
, we have

\displaystyle|

\displaystyle\alpha_{f}(j^{{}^{\prime}},l^{{}^{\prime}},w^{{}^{\prime}})-\hat{\alpha}_{f}(j^{{}^{\prime}},l^{{}^{\prime}},w^{{}^{\prime}})|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\sum\limits_{j=0}^{\log_{2}(\delta^{-1})}\sum\limits_{l=1}^{2^{p}}\sum\limits_{w\in\{-K2^{j},...,K2^{j}\}^{p}}\hat{\alpha}_{f}(j,l,w)\langle\hat{\psi}_{jlw},\psi_{j^{{}^{\prime}}l^{{}^{\prime}}w^{{}^{\prime}}}\rangle-\hat{\alpha}_{f}(j^{{}^{\prime}},l^{{}^{\prime}},w^{{}^{\prime}})|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|\sum\limits_{(j,l,w)\neq(j^{{}^{\prime}},l^{{}^{\prime}},w^{{}^{\prime}})}|\hat{\alpha}_{f}(j,l,w)\langle\hat{\psi}_{jlw},\psi_{j^{{}^{\prime}}l^{{}^{\prime}}w^{{}^{\prime}}}\rangle|

\displaystyle+|\hat{\alpha}_{f}(j^{{}^{\prime}},l^{{}^{\prime}},w^{{}^{\prime}})||\langle\hat{\psi}_{j^{{}^{\prime}}l^{{}^{\prime}}w^{{}^{\prime}}},\psi_{j^{{}^{\prime}}l^{{}^{\prime}}w^{{}^{\prime}}}\rangle-1|\mathds{1}_{\{j^{{}^{\prime}}\leq\log_{2}(\delta^{-1})\}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}(j,l,w)\neq(j^{{}^{\prime}},l^{{}^{\prime}},w^{{}^{\prime}})\\
supp(\hat{\psi}_{jlw})\cap supp(\psi_{j^{{}^{\prime}}l^{{}^{\prime}}w^{{}^{\prime}}})\neq\varnothing\end{subarray}}C2^{-j(\eta+p/2)}2^{(j-j^{{}^{\prime}})(\lfloor\beta\rfloor+2+p/2)}L^{-1}

\displaystyle+C2^{-j^{{}^{\prime}}(\eta+p/2)}L^{-1}\mathds{1}_{\{j^{{}^{\prime}}\leq\log_{2}(\delta^{-1})\}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle CL^{-1}2^{-j^{{}^{\prime}}(\lfloor\beta\rfloor+2+p/2)}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}(j,l,w)\in\{1,...,\log_{2}(\delta^{-1})\}\times\{1,...,2^{p}\}\times\{-K2^{j},...,K2^{j}\}^{p}\\
supp(\hat{\psi}_{jlw})\cap supp(\psi_{j^{{}^{\prime}}l^{{}^{\prime}}w^{{}^{\prime}}})\neq\varnothing\end{subarray}}2^{j(\lfloor\beta\rfloor+2-\eta)}

\displaystyle+C2^{-j^{{}^{\prime}}(\eta+p/2)}L^{-1}\mathds{1}_{\{j^{{}^{\prime}}\leq\log_{2}(\delta^{-1})\}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle CL^{-1}2^{-j^{{}^{\prime}}(\lfloor\beta\rfloor+2+p/2)}\sum\limits_{j\in\{1,...,\log_{2}(\delta^{-1})\}}2^{j(\lfloor\beta\rfloor+2-\eta)}

\displaystyle+C2^{-j^{{}^{\prime}}(\eta+p/2)}L^{-1}\mathds{1}_{\{j^{{}^{\prime}}\leq\log_{2}(\delta^{-1})\}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle CL^{-1}(2^{-j^{{}^{\prime}}(\lfloor\beta\rfloor+2+p/2)}\delta^{-(\lfloor\beta\rfloor+2-\eta)}\log_{2}(\delta^{-1})+2^{-j^{{}^{\prime}}(\eta+p/2)}\mathds{1}_{\{j^{{}^{\prime}}\leq\log_{2}(\delta^{-1})\}}).

∎

A.2.2
Implementation of the class
\hat{\mathcal{F}}^{\beta+1,n^{-\frac{1}{2\beta+d}}}_{per}
(
3.2.2
)

Let us first give a method to find a tanh (or ReQU) neural network
\hat{\phi}
from (
A.4
) that approximates the true scaling function at a precision
n^{-1}
. This method is largely inspired by the Section 4 of
Daubechies and Lagarias 1991
. The idea is to use an iterative scheme based on the two-scale difference equation (
A.3
). The unique solution
\phi
of this equation, is a fixed point fixed point
Vf=f
of the linear operator

Vf(x)=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{N}h_{k}f(2x-k).

(A.11)

Take a function
f_{0}\in\mathcal{H}^{\lfloor\beta\rfloor+3}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R})
such that
f_{0}^{(l)}(k)=\phi^{(l)}(k)
for all
k\in\{0,1,...,N\}
and
l\in\{0,...,\lfloor\beta\rfloor+3\}
. Then, writing
V^{j}f_{0}
for the application of the operator
V
a total of
j
times on the function
f_{0}
, Theorem
4.2
in
Daubechies and Lagarias 1991
states that:

\text{For }f_{j}=V^{j}f_{0},\text{ we have }\|f_{j}^{(l)}-\phi^{(l)}\|_{\infty}\leq C2^{-j(\lfloor\beta\rfloor+3-l)},\forall l\in\{0,...,\lfloor\beta\rfloor+3\}.

We use a smooth neural networks (either with tanh or ReQU activation functions) to approximate the function
f_{0}
. Based on the data
(k,\phi^{(l)}(k))_{k\in\{0,...,N\}}
(
Daubechies and Lagarias 1992
give a precise method to compute the values
\phi^{(l)}(k)
), find a tanh (or ReQU) neural network
\hat{f}_{0}\in\mathcal{R}^{\lfloor\beta\rfloor+3}_{CN}
using classical gradient descent optimization on the weights of the network, such that

\sum\limits_{l=0}^{\lfloor\beta\rfloor+3}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{N}|\hat{f}_{0}^{(l)}(k)-\phi^{(l)}(k)|\leq n^{-\log_{2}(C_{h})-1},

(A.12)

for
C_{h}=\max\limits_{k\in\{0,...,N\}}|h_{k}|\in(1,2)
. Then, the neural network
\hat{f}_{j}=V^{j}\hat{f}_{0}
, which is a linear combination of scalings and translations of
\hat{f}_{0}
, verifies

\|\hat{f}_{j}^{(l)}-\phi^{(l)}\|_{\infty}\leq C\left(2^{-j(\lfloor\beta\rfloor+3-l)}+C_{h}^{j}n^{-\log_{2}(C_{h})-1}\right),\forall l\in\{0,...,\lfloor\beta\rfloor+3\}.

Finally, for
j=\log_{2}(n)
we obtain a tanh (or ReQU) neural network
\hat{\phi}=\hat{f}_{j}
such that

\|\phi-\hat{\phi}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{\lfloor\beta\rfloor+2}}\leq Cn^{-1}.

Note that as we need a smooth approximation of
\phi
, we cannot use classical ReLU neural networks as it will not give us a function that also approximates the derivatives of
\phi
.
Having the approximation
\hat{\phi}
of the scaling function, we can compute the approximated periodised wavelets
\hat{\psi}^{per}_{jlz}
as described in the precedent Section (
A.10
). The class
\hat{\mathcal{F}}^{\beta+1,n^{-\frac{1}{2\beta+d}}}_{per}
(
3.2.2
) can then be implemented as the set of neural networks being linear combinations of the
\hat{\psi}^{per}_{jlz}
, for
j\in\{0,...,\log_{2}(n^{\frac{1}{2\beta+d}})\}
,
l\in\{1,...,2^{d}\}
and
z\in\{0,...,2^{j}-1\}^{d}
.

Let us sum up the different steps of the construction and then detail their tractability.

1.

Find by gradient descent a tanh (or ReQU) neural network
\hat{f}_{0}\in\mathcal{R}^{\lfloor\beta\rfloor+3}_{CN}
verifying (
A.12
).

2.

Compute the neural network

\hat{f}_{j}=V^{j}\hat{f}_{0}=\sum\limits_{k_{1},...,k_{j}\in\{0,...,N\}^{j}}h_{k_{1}}...h_{k_{j}}\ \hat{f}_{0}(2^{j}x-\sum\limits_{i=1}^{j}2^{i-1}k_{i})

for
j=\log_{2}(n).

3.

Build
p
neural networks
f_{i}
of the shape
f_{i}=\sum\limits_{j=0}^{\log_{2}(n^{\frac{1}{2\beta+d}})}\sum\limits_{l=1}^{2^{d}}\sum\limits_{z\in\{0,...,2^{j}-1\}^{d}}\hat{\alpha}_{f}(j,l,z)_{i}\hat{\psi}_{jlz}^{per}
, for
\hat{\psi}^{per}_{jlz}
from (
A.10
).

Step 1.
Although we need to compute the derivatives of
\hat{f}_{0}
up to the order
\lfloor\beta\rfloor+3
, this is not very costly as
\hat{f}_{0}
is a uni-dimensional neural network with width and depth that do not depend on the number of data. Furthermore, we only need to compute the error with
\phi^{(l)}
at the points
k\in\{1,...,N\}
which also does not depend on the number of data. Finally we need to obtain an error
O(n^{-\log_{2}(C_{h})-1})
which is strictly greater than
O(n^{-2})
. To the best of our knowledge, there are not existing results on the number of operations it would take for a tanh or ReQU neural network to obtain such error.
